Like with colleagues at school, or with parents, or even at home with my spouse and relatives—I’ll think I’m helping or clarifying something, but I end up talking over people or kind of taking charge before they’re done. Then afterward I realize I didn’t really give them a chance to finish.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. If I’m sitting in an IEP meeting or one of my admin classes, my leg is usually bouncing under the table, or I’m clicking a pen. Sometimes I’m shifting around in the chair so much that people glance over at me before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really have a clear example of careless mistakes from missing details. I’d have to think about it more, but it’s not something I’ve noticed right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t have a specific example that comes to mind about organizing tasks or activities. I’d probably need to think about what you mean by that a little more.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll go into a parent conference or a staff meeting meaning to keep it short, and then I realize I’m giving three examples, telling a classroom story, and throwing out ideas nobody asked for. Sometimes the other person is clearly trying to wrap it up, and I’m still talking.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or were you really active when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was really active. My mother used to joke that in elementary school I was practically teaching the class from my seat, because I was always out of my chair or talking. If I’m sitting in an IEP meeting or one of my admin classes, my leg is usually bouncing under the table, or I’m clicking a pen. Sometimes I’m shifting around in the chair so much that people glance over at me before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really have a clear example of careless mistakes from missing details. I’d have to think about it more, but it’s not something I’ve noticed right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t have a specific example that comes to mind about organizing tasks or activities. I’d probably need to think about what you mean by that a little more.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll go into a parent conference or a staff meeting meaning to keep it short, and then I realize I’m giving three examples, telling a classroom story, and throwing out ideas nobody asked for. Sometimes the other person is clearly trying to wrap it up, and I’m still talking.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or were you really active when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was really active. My mother used to joke that in elementary school I was practically teaching the class from my seat, because I was always out of my chair or talking. I’d cut in before the teacher even finished giving directions. So, yeah, that was there pretty early on—it wasn’t something that just showed up later in life.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of constantly being on the go and having trouble shutting off your activity level throughout your entire life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. What’s been bringing me in lately is that I feel like I’m constantly running on a motor. I finish one thing at school or at home and I’m immediately jumping into the next thing. It’s like I don’t really know how to shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of constantly being on the go and talking more than others ever caused you any problems at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. At work it’s gotten to the point where complaints were serious enough that I was put on a corrective plan. And at home, my spouse says the caregiving routines are harder because everyone is also having to manage my energy, not just the routine itself.\n\nI feel bad about it because I’m usually trying to help, but it feels like I’m damaging trust with my coworkers and my family.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how much that applies to me. I mean, I notice things around me like anyone else, but I can’t say I’ve clearly tracked that as a big issue. Could you say a little more about what you mean by distracted?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I feel like I’m constantly running on a motor. At school and at home, I’ll finish one thing and then immediately jump into the next, and I just can’t seem to shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking things through?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I do things quickly sometimes, but I haven’t really noticed whether I’m doing them without thinking first. Could you give me an example of what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times when you've done something without thinking it through first?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess the main thing is I jump into conversations before I’ve really thought about whether I should. Like with colleagues at school, or with parents, or even at home with my spouse and relatives—I’ll think I’m helping or clarifying something, but I end up talking over people or kind of taking charge before they’re done. Then afterward I realize I didn’t really give them a chance to finish.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. If I’m sitting in an IEP meeting or one of my admin classes, my leg is usually bouncing under the table, or I’m clicking a pen. Sometimes I’m shifting around in the chair so much that people glance over at me before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really have a clear example of careless mistakes from missing details. I’d have to think about it more, but it’s not something I’ve noticed right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t have a specific example that comes to mind about organizing tasks or activities. I’d probably need to think about what you mean by that a little more.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll go into a parent conference or a staff meeting meaning to keep it short, and then I realize I’m giving three examples, telling a classroom story, and throwing out ideas nobody asked for. Sometimes the other person is clearly trying to wrap it up, and I’m still talking.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or were you really active when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was really active. My mother used to joke that in elementary school I was practically teaching the class from my seat, because I was always out of my chair or talking. I’d cut in before the teacher even finished giving directions. So, yeah, that was there pretty early on—it wasn’t something that just showed up later in life.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of constantly being on the go and having trouble shutting off your activity level throughout your entire life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. What’s been bringing me in lately is that I feel like I’m constantly running on a motor. I finish one thing at school or at home and I’m immediately jumping into the next thing. It’s like I don’t really know how to shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of constantly being on the go and talking more than others ever caused you any problems at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. At work it’s gotten to the point where complaints were serious enough that I was put on a corrective plan. I’ll finish something at school or at home and then immediately jump into the next thing, like I can’t really shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant distress or made it hard for you to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. Like with colleagues at school, or with parents, or even at home with my spouse and relatives—I’ll think I’m helping or clarifying something, but I end up talking over people or kind of taking charge before they’re done. Then afterward I realize I didn’t really give them a chance to finish.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. If I’m sitting in an IEP meeting or one of my admin classes, my leg is usually bouncing under the table, or I’m clicking a pen. Sometimes I’m shifting around in the chair so much that people glance over at me before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really have a clear example of careless mistakes from missing details. I’d have to think about it more, but it’s not something I’ve noticed right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t have a specific example that comes to mind about organizing tasks or activities. I’d probably need to think about what you mean by that a little more.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll go into a parent conference or a staff meeting meaning to keep it short, and then I realize I’m giving three examples, telling a classroom story, and throwing out ideas nobody asked for. Sometimes the other person is clearly trying to wrap it up, and I’m still talking.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or were you really active when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was really active. My mother used to joke that in elementary school I was practically teaching the class from my seat, because I was always out of my chair or talking. I’d cut in before the teacher even finished giving directions. So, yeah, that was there pretty early on—it wasn’t something that just showed up later in life.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of constantly being on the go and having trouble shutting off your activity level throughout your entire life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. What’s been bringing me in lately is that I feel like I’m constantly running on a motor. I finish one thing at school or at home and I’m immediately jumping into the next thing. It’s like I don’t really know how to shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of constantly being on the go and talking more than others ever caused you any problems at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. At work it’s gotten to the point where complaints were serious enough that I was put on a corrective plan. And at home, my spouse says the caregiving routines are harder because everyone is also having to manage my energy, not just the routine itself.\n\nI feel bad about it because I’m usually trying to help, but it feels like I’m damaging trust with my coworkers and my family.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how much that applies to me. I mean, I notice things around me like anyone else, but I can’t say I’ve clearly tracked that as a big issue. Could you say a little more about what you mean by distracted?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities, as an adult?\n\nPatient: Yes. I wouldn’t say I’m running or climbing around, exactly, but I do feel like I have to keep moving. Lately it feels like I’m constantly running on a motor. I’ll finish something at school or at home and then immediately jump into the next thing, like I can’t really shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant distress or made it hard for you to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. At work it’s been a real problem — the complaints got serious enough that I was put on a corrective plan. And at home, my spouse says the caregiving routines are harder because people are dealing with my energy on top of everything else.\n\nIt’s distressing because I’m usually trying to help, but I feel like I’m damaging trust with my coworkers and with my family.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main issue is more about having trouble sitting still and being overly active, or about having difficulty focusing on tasks and following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the sitting still and being overly active part. What brings me in is that lately I feel like I’m constantly running on a motor. I’ll finish one thing at school or at home and then I’m immediately on to the next thing, like I can’t really shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with paying attention to details or with controlling your impulses and activity level?\n\nPatient: It’s more controlling my activity level and impulses, I think. The main thing is I feel like I’m always “on,” like I finish one thing at school or at home and then I’m immediately moving to the next. I don’t really feel able to shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble following through on instructions or completing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that’s the main problem for me. I mean, I can usually understand instructions and get tasks done. If things fall apart, it feels less like I didn’t know what to do and more like I’m just... moving too fast or jumping ahead.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these symptoms of being overly active and talkative as a child, or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: Oh, this goes way back. My mother used to joke that in elementary school I was basically teaching the class from my seat because I was always out of my chair, talking, or cutting in before the teacher even finished giving directions. So no, it didn’t just develop later in life. It’s been part of me since I was a kid.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

I wouldn’t say I’m running or climbing around, exactly, but I do feel like I have to keep moving. Lately it feels like I’m constantly running on a motor. I’ll finish something at school or at home and then immediately jump into the next thing, like I can’t really shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant distress or made it hard for you to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. At work it’s been a real problem — the complaints got serious enough that I was put on a corrective plan. And at home, my spouse says the caregiving routines are harder because people are dealing with my energy on top of everything else.\n\nIt’s distressing because I’m usually trying to help, but I feel like I’m damaging trust with my coworkers and with my family.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main issue is more about having trouble sitting still and being overly active, or about having difficulty focusing on tasks and following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the sitting still and being overly active part. What brings me in is that lately I feel like I’m constantly running on a motor. I’ll finish one thing at school or at home and then I’m immediately on to the next thing, like I can’t really shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with paying attention to details or with controlling your impulses and activity level?\n\nPatient: It’s more controlling my activity level and impulses, I think. The main thing is I feel like I’m always “on,” like I finish one thing at school or at home and then I’m immediately moving to the next. I don’t really feel able to shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble following through on instructions or completing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that’s the main problem for me. I mean, I can usually understand instructions and get tasks done. If things fall apart, it feels less like I didn’t know what to do and more like I’m just... moving too fast or jumping ahead.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these symptoms of being overly active and talkative as a child, or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: Oh, this goes way back. My mother used to joke that in elementary school I was basically teaching the class from my seat because I was always out of my chair, talking, or cutting in before the teacher even finished giving directions. So no, it didn’t just develop later in life. It’s been part of me since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity and activity level are equally problematic, or does one cause you more issues than the other?\n\nPatient: I’d say the activity level causes me more trouble. The impulsive part is there, but what really brings me in is feeling like I’m constantly running on a motor. I finish one thing at school or at home and I’m already jumping into the next thing, like I can’t shut myself off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I’m here because I feel like I’m constantly running on a motor. Like with colleagues at school, or with parents, or even at home with my spouse and relatives—I’ll think I’m helping or clarifying something, but I end up talking over people or kind of taking charge before they’re done. If I’m sitting in an IEP meeting or one of my admin classes, my leg is usually bouncing under the table, or I’m clicking a pen. I don’t really have a clear example of careless mistakes from missing details. I don’t have a specific example that comes to mind about organizing tasks or activities. I’ll go into a parent conference or a staff meeting meaning to keep it short, and then I realize I’m giving three examples, telling a classroom story, and throwing out ideas nobody asked for. Sometimes the other person is clearly trying to wrap it up, and I’m still talking.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or were you really active when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was really active. I finish one thing at school or at home and I’m already jumping into the next thing, like I can’t shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes. Could you give me an example of what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Can you think of any times when you've done something without thinking it through first?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess the main thing is I jump into conversations before I’ve really thought about whether I should. Like with colleagues at school, or with parents, or even at home with my spouse and relatives—I’ll think I’m helping or clarifying something, but I end up talking over people or kind of taking charge before they’re done. Then afterward I realize I didn’t really give them a chance to finish.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or fidgety when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. If I’m sitting in an IEP meeting or one of my admin classes, my leg is usually bouncing under the table, or I’m clicking a pen. Sometimes I’m shifting around in the chair so much that people glance over at me before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have difficulty paying attention to details or making careless mistakes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, honestly. I don’t really have a clear example of careless mistakes from missing details. I’d have to think about it more, but it’s not something I’ve noticed right away.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks and activities?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t have a specific example that comes to mind about organizing tasks or activities. I’d probably need to think about what you mean by that a little more.\n\nDoctor: Do you often talk more than others in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. I’ll go into a parent conference or a staff meeting meaning to keep it short, and then I realize I’m giving three examples, telling a classroom story, and throwing out ideas nobody asked for. Sometimes the other person is clearly trying to wrap it up, and I’m still talking.\n\nDoctor: Did you have a lot of energy or were you really active when you were a kid?\n\nPatient: Yes, I was really active. My mother used to joke that in elementary school I was practically teaching the class from my seat, because I was always out of my chair or talking. I’d cut in before the teacher even finished giving directions. So, yeah, that was there pretty early on—it wasn’t something that just showed up later in life.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms of constantly being on the go and having trouble shutting off your activity level throughout your entire life?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. What’s been bringing me in lately is that I feel like I’m constantly running on a motor. I finish one thing at school or at home and I’m immediately jumping into the next thing. It’s like I don’t really know how to shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms of constantly being on the go and talking more than others ever caused you any problems at work, at home, or in your relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes. At work it’s gotten to the point where complaints were serious enough that I was put on a corrective plan. And at home, my spouse says the caregiving routines are harder because everyone is also having to manage my energy, not just the routine itself.\n\nI feel bad about it because I’m usually trying to help, but it feels like I’m damaging trust with my coworkers and my family.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself easily distracted by things around you?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how much that applies to me. I mean, I notice things around me like anyone else, but I can’t say I’ve clearly tracked that as a big issue. Could you say a little more about what you mean by distracted?\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel the need to be physically active, like running, climbing, or engaging in other intense physical activities, as an adult?\n\nPatient: Yes. I wouldn’t say I’m running or climbing around, exactly, but I do feel like I have to keep moving. Lately it feels like I’m constantly running on a motor. I’ll finish something at school or at home and then immediately jump into the next thing, like I can’t really shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms ever caused you significant distress or made it hard for you to function in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. At work it’s been a real problem — the complaints got serious enough that I was put on a corrective plan. And at home, my spouse says the caregiving routines are harder because people are dealing with my energy on top of everything else.\n\nIt’s distressing because I’m usually trying to help, but I feel like I’m damaging trust with my coworkers and with my family.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your main issue is more about having trouble sitting still and being overly active, or about having difficulty focusing on tasks and following through on instructions?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s more the sitting still and being overly active part. What brings me in is that lately I feel like I’m constantly running on a motor. I’ll finish one thing at school or at home and then I’m immediately on to the next thing, like I can’t really shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you have more trouble with paying attention to details or with controlling your impulses and activity level?\n\nPatient: It’s more controlling my activity level and impulses, I think. The main thing is I feel like I’m always “on,” like I finish one thing at school or at home and then I’m immediately moving to the next. I don’t really feel able to shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble following through on instructions or completing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that’s the main problem for me. I mean, I can usually understand instructions and get tasks done. If things fall apart, it feels less like I didn’t know what to do and more like I’m just... moving too fast or jumping ahead.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these symptoms of being overly active and talkative as a child, or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: Oh, this goes way back. It’s been part of me since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity and activity level are equally problematic, or does one cause you more issues than the other?\n\nPatient: I’d say the activity level causes me more trouble. I don’t really feel able to shut it off.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble following through on instructions or completing tasks?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure that’s the main problem for me. I mean, I can usually understand instructions and get tasks done. If things fall apart, it feels less like I didn’t know what to do and more like I’m just... moving too fast or jumping ahead.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these symptoms of being overly active and talkative as a child, or did they develop later in life?\n\nPatient: Oh, this goes way back. My mother used to joke that in elementary school I was basically teaching the class from my seat because I was always out of my chair, talking, or cutting in before the teacher even finished giving directions. So no, it didn’t just develop later in life. It’s been part of me since I was a kid.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your impulsivity and activity level are equally problematic, or does one cause you more issues than the other?\n\nPatient: I’d say the activity level causes me more trouble. The impulsive part is there, but what really brings me in is feeling like I’m constantly running on a motor. I finish one thing at school or at home and I’m already jumping into the next thing, like I can’t shut myself off.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these symptoms at work, at home, and in social situations?\n\nPatient: Yes. At work it’s become a real issue. The complaints got serious enough that I was put on a corrective plan, which was hard to take.\n\nAt home, my spouse says the caregiving routines are harder because everyone has to manage my energy on top of everything else. And socially… I feel like it affects trust. Like with colleagues and family, I’m trying to help, but I end up making things feel more chaotic, and people don’t know if they can count on me the same way.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms have caused significant problems in multiple areas of your life, such as work, home, and social relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not just one area. At work, it’s gotten serious enough that I was put on a corrective plan because of complaints. At home, my spouse says our caregiving routines are harder because people are having to manage my energy on top of everything else. And with colleagues and family, I feel like I’m hurting trust, even when I’m trying to help."
